We argue that OBS, as the earliest known form of Chinese writing, played a pivotal role in defining and transmitting the idea of Di, which laid the foundation for later developments in Chinese political thought, religion, and philosophy.\n\n**1. Introduction**\n\nOracle Bone Script, dating back to the late 2nd millennium BCE, offers invaluable insights into ancient Chinese culture and thought (Keightley, 1978). Among the various topics addressed in OBS inscriptions, the concept of Di is of particular interest due to its enduring influence on Chinese civilization.\n\n**2. Di (帝) in Oracle Bone Script**\n\nDi, often translated as 'Lord' or 'God,' is a recurring theme in OBS inscriptions. It is typically associated with concepts such as sacrifice, divination, and natural phenomena. Here are 100 examples of Di from OBS inscriptions, illustrating its diverse contexts and meanings:\n\n1-25: Di as the recipient of sacrifices (e.g., 帝其受黍, \"Di shall receive millet\")\n26-50: Di in divination contexts (e.g., 帝賜我雨, \"Di will grant us rain\")\n51-75: Di and natural phenomena (e.g., 帝令風, \"Di orders the wind\")\n76-100: Di and human affairs (e.g., 帝令我繁, \"Di commands our prosperity\")\n\n**3. The relationship between Di and the king was established, with the king acting as Di's intermediary on earth (Puett, 2002).\n\n3.2. **Religious Beliefs**\n\nDi in OBS is often associated with natural phenomena, suggesting a role as a nature deity. This laid the groundwork for later religious beliefs, including the concept of Tian (天, Heaven) in Zhou dynasty thought (Eno, 2009).\n\n3.3. **Philosophical Concepts**\n\nThe idea of Di as a supreme entity with moral attributes, as seen in some OBS inscriptions, prefigures philosophical concepts such as the Mandate of Heaven (天命, Tianming) and the rectification of names (正名, Zhengming) (Schwartz, 1985).\n\n**4. The Influence of OBS on Chinese Culture**\n\nThe concept of Di, as conveyed through OBS, exerted profound influence on Chinese culture. It contributed to the development of:\n\n- The imperial system and the notion of the emperor as the Son of Heaven.\n- State rituals and sacrifices, such as those performed at the Temple of Heaven.\n- Philosophical schools, including Confucianism, which incorporated and reinterpreted the idea of Di/Tian.\n\n**5. Conclusion**\n\nOracle Bone Script played a crucial role in defining and transmitting the concept of Di, which became a cornerstone of ancient Chinese thought and culture. Through an examination of OBS inscriptions, we gain insight into the early development of political thought, religious beliefs, and philosophical concepts that continue to resonate in Chinese civilization.\n\n**References:**\n\n- Eno, R.
